# EXPANSION CW125-05 — Vigilance Remains

## A prose-first memorial expansion about the remembered claim, the people who inherit it, and the blanks that remain human.

### Prose Wave 125: What a Warning Cannot Finish

## Batch brief

**Content type:** original game-content proposal with full scene, attributable writing, conversation, and conditional-return drafts.
**Content bank:** 48 beat pairings; four alternative authored forms for each pairing; 192 candidates, of which a verified consumer should select only what it can support.
**Current local anchor:** memorial_disease_outbreak/vigilance_remains — Disease Outbreak Victims.
**Tone:** A memorial about a remembered outbreak and the uneasy inheritance of vigilance, with no diagnosis or clinical explanation supplied.
**Scope:** narrative prose only; no production code, authoritative JSON edits, mechanics, route, quest, flag, simulation, or save change.

## 1. Expansion thesis

This plan explores A memorial about a remembered outbreak and the uneasy inheritance of vigilance, with no diagnosis or clinical explanation supplied. It uses the source text “The sickness came and took them. We tried to save them, but the disease was too strong. Their memory lives on in our vigilance.” as a narrow canon anchor, not as an excuse to manufacture witnesses or explain every silence. Six movements and forty-eight beat pairings explore how different present readers may respond to the wording. The proposed passages remain optional editorial drafts until a current owner and route are verified.

## 2. Story question

How can vigilance be remembered as care without using an unnamed illness to generate fear, blame, medical instructions, or certainty the catalog does not contain?

## 5. Human center

A caring community’s retrospective sentence: it tried to save people, yet the memorial offers no account of what care meant or what anyone knew.
 The deceased or collective subject must never become a device whose only purpose is to teach a clean lesson to the living. Keep the person or people larger than the inscription’s most repeatable line.

## 6. Voice and point of view

- **A person preserving the memorial wording:** Wants care to remain visible without claiming expertise absent from the source.
- **A reader who asks what vigilance means:** Separates ongoing attention from suspicion of other people.
- **A quiet listener:** Remembers that unnamed victims are not a warning prop.

The people listed here are proposed present-day readers, not canon characters and not witnesses unless another verified source establishes that history. Keep the original memorial voice distinct from any reply written for this plan.

## 7. Placement and current reachability

The exact local anchor is memorial_disease_outbreak/vigilance_remains. MemorialSystem owns survivor memorialization, grief, mourning, and eulogies, but this separate data record’s loader and player-facing consumer have not been verified. Before content placement, confirm the current schema, owner, route, and presentation context. Catalog presence alone proves no playable route. This plan contributes no new route or content registry.

## 8. Player agency

The player may read, ask who authored a claim, hear a second interpretation, decline to discuss it, or leave without a verdict. These are editorial postures rather than promised controls. Silence must remain a complete response. Do not attach approval, trust, reputation, item, resource, treatment, memorial completion, or relationship state to the reader’s interpretation.

## 9. Continuity, dignity, and safety

Do not name the illness, identify patients, infer contagion, symptoms, cause, procedure, treatment, medical outcome, or responsible person. Do not provide medical advice or use ambiguity to create a scare. “Too strong” is the memorial’s judgment, not a clinical finding.
 Keep the source statement, a proposed speaker’s interpretation, and any verified world fact visibly separate. Avoid reenacting death, assigning fault without evidence, or converting grief into a tutorial.

## 11. Narrative sequence

These six editorial movements organize the prose bank; they are not six required visits, a chronology, or a quest chain. A verified consumer may use a single beat, a short excerpt, or none of the candidates.

### Movement 1: The Sickness Came

The record’s plain phrase offers no diagnosis or origin. In this movement the candidate question is: What can a sentence say about illness while leaving medical certainty absent? The memorial’s phrase “the sickness came” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 2: The Ones Taken

Collective loss is named without a patient list. In this movement the candidate question is: How do we keep people present without manufacturing case histories? The memorial’s phrase “took them” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 3: We Tried to Save Them

The living speak as a collective, but the record does not document each act. In this movement the candidate question is: How can care be honored without converting it into a technical claim? The memorial’s phrase “we tried to save them” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 4: Too Strong

The memorial gives a conclusion with no clinical measure. In this movement the candidate question is: What does the phrase explain emotionally and what can it not establish? The memorial’s phrase “the disease was too strong” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 5: Memory Lives On

The text gives memory a continuing role. In this movement the candidate question is: Can memory continue without turning fear toward the living? The memorial’s phrase “their memory lives on” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 6: Vigilance

The final word can mean attention or suspicion. In this movement the candidate question is: How should a later reader keep vigilance humane? The memorial’s phrase “in our vigilance” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

## 12. Beat bank and alternative passage drafts

Every movement meets each of eight source phrases. The four forms per pairing are alternatives: an observable scene, an attributable reader-authored fragment, a two-voice conversation, and a conditional return. Their headings help writers compare form; they do not promise that all versions fit one consumer. Dialogue is deliberately from proposed present readers, not from the person memorialized.

### Beat 01: The Sickness Came × the sickness came

**Beat question:** When a reader meets “the sickness came” during “The Sickness Came,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that a description of arrival, not a diagnosis or causal account?

#### Scene draft 001 — the sickness came — The Sickness Came

This version starts in the small interval between recognizing the record and deciding what to repeat from it. The dramatic work of “The Sickness Came” comes from placing “the sickness came” beside what the memorial does not say. No invented detail is allowed to close that gap. The proposed reader is not a witness to the life being memorialized; their presence only gives the source wording a present audience.

Let the focus settle on “the sickness came”. The phrase has force because a description of arrival, not a diagnosis or causal account. A second person notices that force before anyone turns it into an explanation. They may look toward the line, then toward the person who has stopped reading, giving the beat a human action rather than an invented flashback.

A keeper who sees vigilance as compassion rather than suspicion begins with a modest response: “I can read the sickness came, but I cannot tell from this sentence everything that happened. I want to keep the wording because it matters to someone, and I want to leave room where the record leaves room.” The line is written as present interpretation, not a quotation falsely attributed to the person on the memorial.

A listener who asks for the names the catalog cannot provide replies, “Then let us ask what it asks of us now. When a reader meets “the sickness came” during “The Sickness Came,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that a description of arrival, not a diagnosis or causal account? I can honor the claim and still notice what it does not tell us.” The answer should be specific to the source phrase; it must not flatten the disagreement into a lesson about all grief or all survivors.

A third voice, a reader careful not to impersonate a clinician, may interrupt gently. They do not correct the catalog or add evidence. They ask whether the word “the sickness came” is being repeated because it comforts the room, because it warns the room, or because no one knows what else to say. Let more than one motive remain possible.

The scene’s emotional turn around “the sickness came” in “The Sickness Came” is small: one listener stops trying to finish the missing story for a moment. A chair shifts, a copied line is turned face down and then face up again, or the speaker chooses a less certain verb. The selected gesture should fit the verified presentation context; no physical memorial site is established by this proposal.

For “the sickness came” in “The Sickness Came,” player-facing tone stays open. The player may read, ask for the source wording again, decline the conversation, or simply listen. These are writing possibilities, not a promised menu or branching system. No answer earns approval, trust, resources, safety, reputation, or a new relationship state.

The closing breath belongs to the living reader; do not fabricate an answer on behalf of the person commemorated. The last attention returns to “the sickness came”, now heard through the live disagreement rather than treated as a complete biography.

**Drafting focus.** Keep the reader’s attention on “the sickness came” rather than on a newly invented biography. The writer may ask When a reader meets “the sickness came” during “The Sickness Came,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that a description of arrival, not a diagnosis or causal account? The answer should emerge through voice, hesitation, and what the speakers decline to claim; it should not be delivered by an omniscient narrator. This restraint is part of the content, not a substitute for dramatic movement.

## 13. Player-facing tone

Keep sentences close to the source wording and the present reader’s actual knowledge. Allow one voice to value the memorial and another to object to its universal-sounding conclusion. Neither reaction should be framed as the correct morality. Use quiet material action rather than explanatory narration. Avoid turning illness, hunger, conflict, radiation, cold, machinery, duty, or trust into a puzzle tutorial or instruction to the player.

## 14. Editorial acceptance

Do not name the illness, identify patients, infer contagion, symptoms, cause, procedure, treatment, medical outcome, or responsible person. Do not provide medical advice or use ambiguity to create a scare. “Too strong” is the memorial’s judgment, not a clinical finding.
 Re-check the exact local record and any newer narrative data before selecting text. Retain only passages that the current consumer can reach and attribute honestly. Shortening a candidate is preferable to implying unsupported canon.

## 15. Character and relationship continuity

The proposed roles are anonymous present-day readers. Do not promote them into named survivors, historical witnesses, patients, faction leaders, victims, or permanent companions without a separate source and authorized content decision. Their relationships exist only within the selected text. Gratitude, skepticism, grief, forgiveness, caution, and disagreement do not become saved relationship values.

## 16. Passage-selection map

**Use a scene** when a verified reader-facing owner can stage a present response without claiming a sourced location. **Use a record** when existing content supports authorship and provenance. **Use a conversation** when a second perspective adds a genuine question rather than repeating the thesis. **Use a return vignette** only if the existing route already supports the callback condition; otherwise use its non-conditional final line or omit it. These are writing choices, not feature requests.

## 17. Content boundaries and open questions

| Topic | Current evidence | Editorial limit |
|---|---|---|
| Anchor | memorial_disease_outbreak in the memorial catalog | Presence does not prove a live route or consumer. |
| Public wording | The sickness came and took them. We tried to save them, but the disease was too strong. Their memory lives on in our vigilance. | Preserve exact text and separate it from interpretation. |
| Proposed voices | Anonymous roles in this document | Do not claim they are witnesses or canonical characters. |
| Reader response | Four prose alternatives per beat | No new menu, flag, score, reward, relationship, or save behavior. |
| Sensitive subject | tragedy and tags ['disease', 'tragedy', 'memory'] | No operational guidance, diagnosis, guarantee, or unsupported outcome. |

Do not name the illness, identify patients, infer contagion, symptoms, cause, procedure, treatment, medical outcome, or responsible person. Do not provide medical advice or use ambiguity to create a scare. “Too strong” is the memorial’s judgment, not a clinical finding.
 Every passage is editorial. The questions of authorship, setting, timing, memory, and consumer remain open until checked against current code and narrative data.
